\left\|\delta_{t_{\epsilon}}\right\|_{2}\leq C_{1}\tilde{d}^{-1/4}
. Then, for
t>t_{\epsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\delta_{t}\right\|_{2}-\left\|\delta_{t_{\epsilon}}\right\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle\eta\sum_{s=t_{\epsilon}}^{t-1}\left\|\left[\nabla_{\theta}f(x;\tilde{\theta}^{(s)})^{\top}J(\theta^{(s)})-\nabla_{\theta}f(x;\theta^{(0)})^{\top}J(\theta^{(0)})\right]\sqrt{{\bm{Q}}_{3\epsilon}^{(s)}}\right\|_{2}\left\|\sqrt{{\bm{Q}}}g(\theta^{(s)})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\eta\sum_{s=t_{\epsilon}}^{t-1}\left\|\nabla_{\theta}f(x;\theta^{(0)})^{\top}J(\theta^{(0)})\sqrt{{\bm{Q}}_{3\epsilon}^{(s)}}\right\|_{2}\left\|\sqrt{{\bm{Q}}}\Delta_{s}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\eta M^{2}C_{0}\tilde{d}^{-1/4}\sqrt{1+3\epsilon}\sum_{s=t_{\epsilon}}^{t-1}\gamma^{s-t_{\epsilon}}B^{t_{\epsilon}}R_{0}

\displaystyle+\eta M^{2}\sqrt{1+3\epsilon}\sum_{s=t_{\epsilon}}^{t-1}\left(2\gamma^{s-t_{\epsilon}}\eta M^{2}C_{0}B^{t_{\epsilon}}R_{0}\left[t_{\epsilon}B^{-1}+\sqrt{1+3\epsilon}\gamma^{-1}(s-t_{\epsilon})\right]\tilde{d}^{-1/4}\right).

Note that
\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}t\gamma^{t}
is finite as long as
\gamma\in(0,1)
. Therefore, there is a constant
C
such that for any
t
,
\left\|\delta_{t}\right\|_{2}\leq C\tilde{d}^{-1/4}
with probability at least
(1-\delta)
for any
\tilde{d}\geq\tilde{D}
.

Now, let us finish the proof of
Theorem

6.6
.

Proof

Consider the linearized neural network
f_{\textnormal{lin}}^{(t)}(x)
defined in
Equation

29
.
If we view
{\left\{\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x_{i})\right\}}_{i=1}^{n}
as inputs and
{\left\{y_{i}-f^{(0)}(x_{i})+{\left\langle\theta^{(0)},\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x_{i})\right\rangle}\right\}}_{i=1}^{n}
as the targets, then the linearized neural network is a linear model. Thus by
Theorem

6.4
, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 12.6
.

If
\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x_{1}),\cdots,\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x_{n})
are linearly independent, then there exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that when
f_{\textnormal{lin}}^{(t)}(x)
is trained with any GRW method that satisfies
Assumption

6.3
,
\theta^{(t)}
converges to a unique
\theta^{*}
that does not depend on the sample weights
q_{i}^{(t)}
.

Let
\eta_{1}=\min\{\eta_{0},\eta^{*}\}
, where
\eta_{0}
is given by
Corollary

12.6
.
Let
f^{(t)}_{\textnormal{lin}}(x)
and
f^{(t)}_{\textnormal{lin}\textnormal{ERM}}(x)
be the linearized neural networks of
f^{(t)}(x)
and
f^{(t)}_{\textnormal{ERM}}(x)
(which are two networks trained with GRW and ERM), respectively.
By
Lemma

12.2
, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, there exists
\tilde{D}>0
and a constant
C
such that with probability at least
1-\delta
, the following holds:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sup_{t\geq 0}\left|f^{(t)}_{\textnormal{lin}}(x)-f^{(t)}(x)\right|\leq C\tilde{d}^{-1/4};\\
&\sup_{t\geq 0}\left|f^{(t)}_{\textnormal{lin}\textnormal{ERM}}(x)-f^{(t)}_{\textnormal{ERM}}(x)\right|\leq C\tilde{d}^{-1/4}.\end{aligned}\right.

By
Corollary

12.6
, we have

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\left|f^{(t)}_{\textnormal{lin}}(x)-f^{(t)}_{\textnormal{lin}\textnormal{ERM}}(x)\right|=0.

Summing the above yields

\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\left|f^{(t)}(x)-f^{(t)}_{\textnormal{ERM}}(x)\right|\leq 2C\tilde{d}^{-1/4},

as desired.

Appendix 12.C
Proof of Theorem
6.7

Similar to
Theorem

6.6
, proving this result needs a slightly different approximation theorem. We start with two necessary propositions.

Proposition 12.7

(
[
77
]
, Proposition 1)
.

If
\sigma
is Lipschitz and
d_{l}\rightarrow\infty
for
l=1,\cdots,L
sequentially, then for all
l=1,\cdots,L
, the distribution of a single element of
{\bm{h}}^{l}
converges in probability to a zero-mean Gaussian process of covariance
\Sigma^{l}
that is defined recursively by:

\displaystyle\Sigma^{1}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d_{0}}x^{\top}x^{\prime}+\beta^{2};

\displaystyle\Sigma^{l}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{f}[\sigma(f(x))\sigma(f(x^{\prime}))]+\beta^{2},

where
f
is sampled from a zero-mean Gaussian process of covariance
\Sigma^{(l-1)}
.

Proposition 12.8
.

For any positive definite symmetric matrix
{\bm{H}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, denote its largest and smallest eigenvalues by
\lambda_{\max}
and
\lambda_{\min}
. Then, for any positive semi-definite diagonal matrix
{\bm{Q}}={\textnormal{diag}}(q_{1},\cdots,q_{n})
,
{\bm{H}}{\bm{Q}}
has
n
eigenvalues that all lie in
[\min_{i}q_{i}\cdot\lambda_{\min},\max_{i}q_{i}\cdot\lambda_{\max}]
.

Proof

{\bm{H}}
is a positive definite symmetric matrix, so there exists
{\bm{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that
{\bm{H}}={\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}
, and
{\bm{A}}
is full-rank. First, any eigenvalue of
{\bm{A}}{\bm{Q}}{\bm{A}}^{\top}
is also an eigenvalue of
{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}{\bm{Q}}
, because for any eigenvalue
\lambda
of
{\bm{A}}{\bm{Q}}{\bm{A}}^{\top}
we have some
{\bm{v}}\neq 0
such that
{\bm{A}}{\bm{Q}}{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{v}}=\lambda{\bm{v}}
. Multiplying both sides by
{\bm{A}}^{\top}
on the left yields
{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}{\bm{Q}}({\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{v}})=\lambda({\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{v}})
which implies that
\lambda
is also an eigenvalue of
{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}{\bm{Q}}
because
{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{v}}\neq 0
as
\lambda{\bm{v}}\neq 0
.

Second, by condition we know that the eigenvalues of
{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}
are all in
[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}]
where
\lambda_{\min}>0
, which implies for any unit vector
{\bm{v}}
,
{\bm{v}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}{\bm{v}}\in[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}]
, which is equivalent to
\left\|{\bm{A}}{\bm{v}}\right\|_{2}\in[\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}},\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}}]
.
Thus,
{\bm{v}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{Q}}{\bm{A}}{\bm{v}}\in[\lambda_{\min}\min_{i}q_{i},\lambda_{\max}\max_{i}q_{i}]
, which implies that the eigenvalues of
{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{Q}}{\bm{A}}
are all in
[\lambda_{\min}\min_{i}q_{i},\lambda_{\max}\max_{i}q_{i}]
.

Thus, the eigenvalues of
{\bm{H}}{\bm{Q}}={\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}{\bm{Q}}
are all in
[\lambda_{\min}\min_{i}q_{i},\lambda_{\max}\max_{i}q_{i}]
.

Lemma 12.9

(Approximation Theorem for Regularized GRW)
.

For a wide fully-connected neural network
f
, denote
J(\theta)=\nabla_{\theta}f({\bm{X}};\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
and
g(\theta)=\nabla_{\hat{y}}\ell(f({\bm{X}};\theta),{\bm{Y}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Given that the loss function
\ell
satisfies:
\nabla_{\theta}g(\theta)=J(\theta){\bm{U}}(\theta)
for any
\theta
, and
{\bm{U}}(\theta)
is a positive semi-definite diagonal matrix whose elements are uniformly bounded, we have: for any GRW that minimizes the regularized weighted empirical risk
Equation

30
with a sufficiently small learning rate
\eta
, there is: for a sufficiently large
\tilde{d}
, with high probability over random initialization, on any test point
x
such that
\|x\|_{2}\leq 1
, we have

\sup_{t\geq 0}\left|f^{(t)}_{\textnormal{lin}\textrm{reg}}(x)-f^{(t)}_{\textrm{reg}}(x)\right|\leq C\tilde{d}^{-1/4},

(54)

where both
f^{(t)}_{\textnormal{lin}\textrm{reg}}
and
f^{(t)}_{\textrm{reg}}
are trained by the same regularized GRW and start from the same initial point.

Proof

Without loss of generality, assume that all elements of
{\bm{U}}(\theta)
are in
[0,1]
for all
\theta
, and set
\eta\leq(\mu+\lambda_{\min}+\lambda_{\max})^{-1}
.
If the elements of
{\bm{U}}(\theta)
are bounded by
[0,C]
, then we can set
\eta\leq(\mu+C\lambda_{\min}+C\lambda_{\max})^{-1}
and prove the result in the same way.

With
L_{2}
penalty, the update rule of the GRW for the neural network is:

\theta^{(t+1)}=\theta^{(t)}-\eta J(\theta^{(t)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}g(\theta^{(t)})-\eta\mu(\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{(0)}).

(55)

And the update rule for the linearized neural network is:

\theta^{(t+1)}_{\textnormal{lin}}=\theta^{(t)}_{\textnormal{lin}}-\eta J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}g(\theta_{\textnormal{lin}}^{(t)})-\eta\mu(\theta_{\textnormal{lin}}^{(t)}-\theta^{(0)}).

(56)

By
Proposition

12.7
,
f(x;\theta)
converges in probability to a zero-mean Gaussian process. Thus, for any
\delta>0
, there exists a constant
R_{0}>0
such that with probability at least
(1-\delta/3)
,
\left\|g(\theta^{(0)})\right\|_{2}<R_{0}
.
Let
M
be given by
Lemma

12.4
. Denote
A=\eta MR_{0}
, and let
C_{0}=\frac{4A}{\eta\mu}
be given by
Lemma

12.4
. Note that
Lemma

12.4
only depends on the network structure, but does not depend on the update rule, so we can still use the lemma here.
By
Lemma

12.4
, there exists
D_{1}
such that for all
\tilde{d}\geq D_{1}
, with probability at least
(1-\delta/3)
,
Equation

45
holds.

Similar to the proof of
Proposition

12.8
, we can show that for any
\tilde{\theta}
, all the non-zero eigenvalues of
J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}U(\tilde{\theta})J(\theta^{(0)})^{\top}
are also eigenvalues of
J(\theta^{(0)})^{\top}J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}U(\tilde{\theta})
.
This is because for any eigenvalue
\lambda\neq 0
such that
J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}U(\tilde{\theta})J(\theta^{(0)})^{\top}{\bm{v}}=\lambda{\bm{v}}
for some
{\bm{v}}
, we must have
J(\theta^{(0)})^{\top}J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}U(\tilde{\theta})(J(\theta^{(0)})^{\top}{\bm{v}})=\lambda(J(\theta^{(0)})^{\top}{\bm{v}})
, and
J(\theta^{(0)})^{\top}{\bm{v}}\neq 0
since
\lambda{\bm{v}}\neq 0
, so
\lambda
is also an eigenvalue of
J(\theta^{(0)})^{\top}J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}U(\tilde{\theta})
.
On the other hand, by
Lemma

6.5
,
J(\theta^{(0)})^{\top}J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}U(\tilde{\theta})
converges in probability to
\Theta{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}U(\tilde{\theta})
whose eigenvalues are all in
[0,\lambda_{\max}]
by
Proposition

12.8
.
Hence, there exists
D_{2}
such that for all
\tilde{d}\geq D_{2}
, with probability at least
(1-\delta/3)
, the eigenvalues of
J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}U(\tilde{\theta})J(\theta^{(0)})^{\top}
are all in
[0,\lambda_{\max}+\lambda_{\min}]
for all
t
.

By union bound, with probability at least
1-\delta
, all the above “
(1-\delta/3)
” statements are true.
Now we prove that there exists
D_{0}
such that for all
\tilde{d}\geq D_{0}
,
\sup_{t\geq 0}\left\|\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{(0)}\right\|_{2}
is bounded with high probability. Denote
a_{t}=\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{(0)}
.
By
Equation

55
, we have

\displaystyle a_{t+1}=

\displaystyle(1-\eta\mu)a_{t}-\eta[J(\theta^{(t)})-J(\theta^{(0)})]{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}g(\theta^{(t)})

\displaystyle-\eta J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}[g(\theta^{(t)})-g(\theta^{(0)})]-\eta J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}g(\theta^{(0)}),

which implies that

\displaystyle\left\|a_{t+1}\right\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle\left\|(1-\eta\mu){\bm{I}}-\eta J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}U(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})J(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})^{\top}\right\|_{2}\left\|a_{t}\right\|_{2}

(57)

\displaystyle+\eta\left\|J(\theta^{(t)})-J(\theta^{(0)})\right\|_{F}\left\|g(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}+\eta\left\|J(\theta^{(0)})\right\|_{F}\left\|g(\theta^{(0)})\right\|_{2},

where
\tilde{\theta}^{(t)}
is some linear interpolation between
\theta^{(t)}
and
\theta^{(0)}
. Our choice of
\eta
ensures that
\eta\mu<1
.

Next, we prove by induction that
{\left\|a_{t}\right\|}_{2}<C_{0}
.
It is true for
t=0
.
Suppose
{\left\|a_{t}\right\|}_{2}<C_{0}
, and consider
a_{t+1}
.
Let us look at the three terms on the right-hand side of
Equation

57
.
For the first term, we have

\displaystyle\left\|(1-\eta\mu){\bm{I}}-\eta J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}U(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})J(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})^{\top}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle(1-\eta\mu)\left\|{\bm{I}}-\frac{\eta}{1-\eta\mu}J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}U(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})J(\theta^{(0)})^{\top}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\eta\left\|J(\theta^{(0)})\right\|_{F}\left\|J(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})-J(\theta^{(0)})\right\|_{F}.

Since
\eta/(1-\eta\mu)\leq(\lambda_{\min}+\lambda_{\max})^{-1}
by our choice of
\eta
, we have

\left\|{\bm{I}}-\frac{\eta}{1-\eta\mu}J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}U(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})J(\theta^{(0)})^{\top}\right\|_{2}\leq 1.

On the other hand, since
\left\|a_{t}\right\|_{2}<C_{0}
, we have
\left\|J(\theta^{(0)})\right\|_{F}\left\|J(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})-J(\theta^{(0)})\right\|_{F}\leq\frac{M^{2}}{\sqrt[4]{\tilde{d}}}C_{0}
by
Equation

45
. Therefore, there exists
D_{3}
such that for all
\tilde{d}\geq D_{3}
,

\left\|(1-\eta\mu){\bm{I}}-\eta J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}U(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})J(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})^{\top}\right\|_{2}\leq 1-\frac{\eta\mu}{2}.

(58)

For the second term, we have

\displaystyle\left\|g(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|g(\theta^{(t)})-g(\theta^{(0)})\right\|_{2}+\left\|g(\theta^{(0)})\right\|_{2}

(59)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|J(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})\right\|_{2}\left\|U(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})\right\|_{2}\left\|\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{(0)}\right\|_{2}+R_{0}\leq MC_{0}+R_{0}.

For the third term, we have

\eta\left\|J(\theta^{(0)})\right\|_{F}\left\|g(\theta^{(0)})\right\|_{2}\leq\eta MR_{0}=A.

Thus, we have

\left\|a_{t+1}\right\|_{2}\leq\left(1-\frac{\eta\mu}{2}\right)\left\|a_{t}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\eta M(MC_{0}+R_{0})}{\sqrt[4]{\tilde{d}}}+A.

Thus, there exists
D_{4}
such that for all
\tilde{d}\geq D_{4}
,
\left\|a_{t+1}\right\|_{2}\leq\left(1-\frac{\eta\mu}{2}\right)\left\|a_{t}\right\|_{2}+2A
. This shows that if
\left\|a_{t}\right\|_{2}<C_{0}
is true, then
\left\|a_{t+1}\right\|_{2}<C_{0}
will also be true.

In conclusion, for all
\tilde{d}\geq D_{0}=\max\{D_{1},D_{2},D_{3},D_{4}\}
,
\left\|\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{(0)}\right\|_{2}<C_{0}
is true for all
t
. This also implies that for
C_{1}=MC_{0}+R_{0}
, we have
\left\|g(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}\leq C_{1}
for all
t
by
Equation

59
. Similarly, we can prove that
\|\theta_{\textnormal{lin}}^{(t)}-\theta^{(0)}\|_{2}<C_{0}
for all
t
.

Second, let
\Delta_{t}=\theta_{\textnormal{lin}}^{(t)}-\theta^{(t)}
. Then, we have

\Delta_{t+1}-\Delta_{t}=\eta(J(\theta^{(t)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}g(\theta^{(t)})-J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}g(\theta_{\textnormal{lin}}^{(t)})-\mu\Delta_{t}),

which implies that

\Delta_{t+1}=\left[(1-\eta\mu){\bm{I}}-\eta J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}U(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})J(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})^{\top}\right]\Delta_{t}+\eta(J(\theta^{(t)})-J(\theta^{(0)})){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}g(\theta^{(t)}),

where
\tilde{\theta}^{(t)}
is some linear interpolation between
\theta^{(t)}
and
\theta_{\textnormal{lin}}^{(t)}
. By
Equation

58
, with probability at least
(1-\delta)
for all
\tilde{d}\geq D_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta_{t+1}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|(1-\eta\mu){\bm{I}}-\eta J(\theta^{(0)}){\bm{Q}}^{(t)}U(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})J(\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})^{\top}\right\|_{2}\left\|\Delta_{t}\right\|_{2}+\eta\left\|J(\theta^{(t)})-J(\theta^{(0)})\right\|_{F}\left\|g(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{\eta\mu}{2}\right)\left\|\Delta_{t}\right\|_{2}+\eta\frac{M}{\sqrt[4]{\tilde{d}}}C_{0}C_{1}.

Again, as
\Delta_{0}=0
, we can prove by induction that for all
t
,

\left\|\Delta_{t}\right\|_{2}<\frac{2MC_{0}C_{1}}{\mu}\tilde{d}^{-1/4}.

For any test point
x
such that
{\left\|x\right\|}_{2}\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\left|f_{\textrm{reg}}^{(t)}(x)-f_{\textnormal{lin}\textrm{reg}}^{(t)}(x)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|f(x;\theta^{(t)})-f_{\textnormal{lin}}(x;\theta^{(t)}_{\textnormal{lin}})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|f(x;\theta^{(t)})-f_{\textnormal{lin}}(x;\theta^{(t)})\right|+\left|f_{\textnormal{lin}}(x;\theta^{(t)})-f_{\textnormal{lin}}(x;\theta^{(t)}_{\textnormal{lin}})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|f(x;\theta^{(t)})-f_{\textnormal{lin}}(x;\theta^{(t)})\right|+\left\|\nabla_{\theta}f(x;\theta^{(0)})\right\|_{2}\left\|\theta^{(t)}-\theta_{\textnormal{lin}}^{(t)}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left|f(x;\theta^{(t)})-f_{\textnormal{lin}}(x;\theta^{(t)})\right|+M\left\|\Delta_{t}\right\|_{2}.

For the first term, note that

\left\{\begin{aligned} f(x;\theta^{(t)})-f(x;\theta^{(0)})&=\nabla_{\theta}f(x;\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})(\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{(0)});\\
f_{\textnormal{lin}}(x;\theta^{(t)})-f_{\textnormal{lin}}(x;\theta^{(0)})&=\nabla_{\theta}f(x;\theta^{(0)})(\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{(0)}),\end{aligned}\right.

where
\tilde{\theta}^{(t)}
is some linear interpolation between
\theta^{(t)}
and
\theta^{(0)}
. Since
f(x;\theta^{(0)})=f_{\textnormal{lin}}(x;\theta^{(0)})
,

\left|f(x;\theta^{(t)})-f_{\textnormal{lin}}(x;\theta^{(t)})\right|\leq\left\|\nabla_{\theta}f(x;\tilde{\theta}^{(t)})-\nabla_{\theta}f(x;\theta^{(0)})\right\|_{2}\left\|\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{(0)}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{M}{\sqrt[4]{\tilde{d}}}C_{0}^{2}.

Thus, for all
\tilde{d}\geq D_{0}
, with probability at least
(1-\delta)
for all
t
and all
x
,

\left|f_{\textrm{reg}}^{(t)}(x)-f_{\textnormal{lin}\textrm{reg}}^{(t)}(x)\right|\leq\left(MC_{0}^{2}+\frac{2M^{2}C_{0}C_{1}}{\mu}\right)\tilde{d}^{-1/4}=O(\tilde{d}^{-1/4}),

which proves the lemma.

Lemma 12.10
.

Suppose there exists
M_{0}>0
such that
\left\|\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x)\right\|_{2}\leq M_{0}
for all
x
within the unit ball.
If the gradients
\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x_{1}),\cdots,\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x_{n})
are linearly independent, and the empirical training risk of
f_{\textnormal{lin}\textrm{reg}}^{(t)}
satisfies
\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\hat{{\mathcal{R}}}(f_{\textnormal{lin}\textrm{reg}}^{(t)})<\epsilon
for some
\epsilon>0
, then for any
x
within the unit ball, we have

\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\left|f_{\textnormal{lin}\textrm{reg}}^{(t)}(x)-f_{\textnormal{lin}\textnormal{ERM}}^{(t)}(x)\right|=O(\sqrt{\epsilon}).

Proof

First, for all
t
we have
\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{(0)}\in{\textnormal{span}}\{\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x_{1}),\cdots,\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x_{n})\}
.
Let
\theta^{*}
be the interpolator in
{\textnormal{span}}(\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x_{1}),\cdots,\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x_{n}))
, then the empirical risk of
\theta
is
\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\theta-\theta^{*},\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x_{i})\rangle^{2}=\frac{1}{2n}\left\|\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}({\bm{X}})^{\top}(\theta-\theta^{*})\right\|_{2}^{2}
.
Thus, there exists
T>0
such that

\left\|\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}({\bm{X}})^{\top}(\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{*})\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2n\epsilon\qquad\text{for all }t\geq T.

Let the smallest singular value of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}({\bm{X}})
be
s_{\min}
.
Then, we have
s_{\min}>0
.
Note that the column space of
\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}({\bm{X}})
is exactly
{\textnormal{span}}(\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x_{1}),\cdots,\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x_{n}))
. Define
{\bm{H}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
such that its columns form an orthonormal basis of this subspace, then there exists
{\bm{G}}\in R^{n\times n}
such that
\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}({\bm{X}})={\bm{H}}{\bm{G}}
, and the smallest singular value of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}{\bm{G}}
is also
s_{\min}
. Since
\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{(0)}
is also in this subspace, there exists
{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{*}={\bm{H}}{\bm{v}}
. Then we have
\sqrt{2n\epsilon}\geq\left\|{\bm{G}}^{\top}{\bm{H}}^{\top}{\bm{H}}{\bm{v}}\right\|_{2}=\left\|{\bm{G}}^{\top}{\bm{v}}\right\|_{2}
. Thus,
\left\|{\bm{v}}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2\epsilon}}{s_{\min}}
, which implies that

\left\|\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2\epsilon}}{s_{\min}}.

We have already proved in the previous results that if we minimize the unregularized risk with ERM, then
\theta
always converges to the interpolator
\theta^{*}
. So for any
t\geq T
and any test point
x
such that
\left\|x\right\|_{2}\leq 1
, we have

|f_{\textnormal{lin}\textrm{reg}}^{(t)}(x)-f_{\textnormal{lin}\textnormal{ERM}}^{(t)}(x)|=|\langle\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{*},\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x)\rangle|\leq\frac{M_{0}\sqrt{2\epsilon}}{s_{\min}},

as desired.

Now we prove
Theorem

6.7
.

Proof

Given that
\hat{{\mathcal{R}}}(f_{\textnormal{lin}\textrm{reg}}^{(t)})<\epsilon
for sufficiently large
t
,
Lemma

12.9
implies that

\left|\hat{{\mathcal{R}}}(f_{\textnormal{lin}\textrm{reg}}^{(t)})-\hat{{\mathcal{R}}}(f_{\textrm{reg}}^{(t)})\right|=O(\tilde{d}^{-1/4}\sqrt{\epsilon}+\tilde{d}^{-1/2}).

Thus, for a fixed
\epsilon
, there exists
D>0
such that for all
d\geq D
, for sufficiently large
t
,

\hat{{\mathcal{R}}}(f_{\textrm{reg}}^{(t)})<\epsilon\Rightarrow\hat{{\mathcal{R}}}(f_{\textnormal{lin}\textrm{reg}}^{(t)})<2\epsilon.

By
Lemma

12.2
and
Lemma

12.9
, we have

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sup_{t\geq 0}\left|f_{\textnormal{lin}\textnormal{ERM}}^{(t)}(x)-f_{\textnormal{ERM}}^{(t)}(x)\right|=O(\tilde{d}^{-1/4});\\
&\sup_{t\geq 0}\left|f_{\textnormal{lin}\textrm{reg}}^{(t)}(x)-f_{\textrm{reg}}^{(t)}(x)\right|=O(\tilde{d}^{-1/4}).\end{aligned}\right.

Combining these with
Lemma

12.10
yields

\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\left|f_{\textrm{reg}}^{(t)}(x)-f_{\textnormal{ERM}}^{(t)}(x)\right|=O(\tilde{d}^{-1/4}+\sqrt{\epsilon}).

Letting
\tilde{d}\rightarrow\infty
leads to the result we need.

Remark 12.11
.

One might wonder whether
\|\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x)\|_{2}
will diverge as
\tilde{d}\rightarrow\infty
.
In fact, in
Lemma

12.4
, we have proved that there exists a constant
M
such that with high probability, for any
\tilde{d}
there is
\|\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x)\|_{2}\leq M
for any
x
such that
\|x\|_{2}\leq 1
. Therefore, it is fine to suppose that there exists such an
M_{0}
.

Appendix 12.D
Proof of Theorem
6.8

Proof

First, we show that
\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}}
is unique.
Suppose both
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
maximize
\min_{i}y_{i}\cdot{\left\langle\theta,{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rangle}
and
{\left\|\theta_{1}\right\|}_{2}={\left\|\theta_{2}\right\|}_{2}=1
.
If
\theta_{1}\neq\theta_{2}
, then we define
\theta=(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})/2
and
\theta_{0}=\theta/{\left\|\theta\right\|}_{2}
. Obviously,
{\left\|\theta\right\|}_{2}<1
, and for all
i\in[n]
there is
y_{i}{\left\langle\theta,{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rangle}=(y_{i}{\left\langle\theta_{1},{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rangle}+y_{i}{\left\langle\theta_{2},{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rangle})/2
.
Thus, we have
y_{i}{\left\langle\theta_{0},{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rangle}>\min{\left\{y_{i}{\left\langle\theta_{1},{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rangle},y_{i}{\left\langle\theta_{2},{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rangle}\right\}}
,
which implies that
\min_{i}y_{i}{\left\langle\theta_{0},{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rangle}>\min{\left\{\min_{i}y_{i}{\left\langle\theta_{1},{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rangle},\min_{i}y_{i}{\left\langle\theta_{2},{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rangle}\right\}}
, which contradicts the fact that
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
are max-margin classifiers.

Without loss of generality, let
({\bm{x}}_{1},y_{1}),\cdots,({\bm{x}}_{m},y_{m})
be the samples with the smallest margin for
{\bm{u}}
, that is
\argmin_{i}y_{i}{\left\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rangle}={\left\{1,\cdots,m\right\}}
.
Denote
\gamma=\min_{i}y_{i}{\left\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rangle}
; then,
\gamma>0
since the training error converges to zero. Note that for the logistic loss, if
y_{i}{\left\langle\theta,{\bm{x}}_{i}\right\rangle}<y_{j}{\left\langle\theta,{\bm{x}}_{j}\right\rangle}
, then for any
M>0
, there exists an
R_{M}>0
such that for all
R\geq R_{M}
, there is
\frac{\nabla_{\theta}\ell(\langle R\theta,{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle,y_{i})}{\nabla_{\theta}\ell(\langle R\theta,{\bm{x}}_{j}\rangle,y_{j})}>M
.
Since the training error converges to zero, we have
{\left\|\theta^{(t)}\right\|}_{2}\rightarrow\infty
.
So when
t
is sufficiently large, the impact of
({\bm{x}}_{j},y_{j})
on
\theta^{(t)}
for
j>m
is an infinitesimal compared to
j\leq m
since
\liminf_{t\rightarrow\infty}q_{j}^{(t)}>0
.
Thus, we must have
{\bm{u}}\in{\textnormal{span}}{\left\{{\bm{x}}_{1},\cdots,{\bm{x}}_{m}\right\}}
.

Let
{\bm{u}}=\alpha_{1}y_{1}{\bm{x}}_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{m}y_{m}{\bm{x}}_{m}
. Now we show that
\alpha_{i}\geq 0
for all
i=1,\cdots,m
.
For a sufficiently large
t
, there is
\theta^{(t+1)}-\theta^{(t)}\approx\eta\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{q_{i}^{(t)}\exp(y_{i}\cdot\langle\theta^{(t)},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle)}{1+\exp(y_{i}\cdot\langle\theta^{(t)},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle)}y_{i}{\bm{x}}_{i}
.
Since
{\left\|\theta^{(t)}\right\|}\rightarrow\infty
, for all
i\in[m]
we have
\alpha_{i}\propto\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{t=T_{0}}^{T}\frac{q_{i}^{(t)}\exp(y_{i}\cdot\langle\theta^{(t)},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle)}{1+\exp(y_{i}\cdot\langle\theta^{(t)},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle)}:=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\alpha_{i}(T)
, where
T_{0}
is sufficiently large.
Here the notion
\alpha_{i}\propto\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\alpha_{i}(T)
means that
\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\alpha_{i}(T)}{\alpha_{j}(T)}=\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\alpha_{j}}
for any pair of
i,j
and
\alpha_{j}\neq 0
. Note that each term in the sum is non-negative. This implies that
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{m}
have the same sign.
Meanwhile,
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}\gamma=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}y_{i}\cdot\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle=\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{u}}\rangle>0
. Thus
\alpha_{i}\geq 0
for all
i\in[m]
and at least one is positive.
Now suppose
{\bm{u}}\neq\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}}
, which means that
\gamma
is smaller than the margin of
\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}}
.
Then, for all
i=1,\cdots,m
, there is
y_{i}\cdot\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle<y_{i}\cdot\langle\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle
. This implies that
\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{u}}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}y_{i}\cdot\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle<\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}y_{i}\cdot\langle\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle=\langle\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}},{\bm{u}}\rangle
,
which is a contradiction. Thus,
{\bm{u}}=\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}}
.

Appendix 12.E
Proof of Theorem
6.10

Proof

Denote the largest and smallest eigenvalues of
{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}
by
\lambda^{\max}
and
\lambda^{\min}
, and by condition we have
\lambda^{\min}>0
. Let
\epsilon=\min\{\frac{q^{*}}{3},\frac{(q^{*}\lambda^{\min})^{2}}{192\lambda^{\max 2}}\}
.
Then, similar to the proof in
Appendix

12.A
, there exists
t_{\epsilon}
such that for all
t\geq t_{\epsilon}
and all
i
,
q_{i}^{(t)}\in(q_{i}-\epsilon,q_{i}+\epsilon)
. Denote
{\bm{Q}}={\textnormal{diag}}(q_{1},\cdots,q_{n})
, then for all
t\geq t_{\epsilon}
,
{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}:={\bm{Q}}_{\epsilon}^{(t)}=\sqrt{{\bm{Q}}}\sqrt{{\bm{Q}}_{3\epsilon}^{(t)}}
, where we use the subscript
\epsilon
to indicate that
\left\|{\bm{Q}}_{\epsilon}^{(t)}-{\bm{Q}}\right\|_{2}<\epsilon
.

First, we prove that
F(\theta)
is
L
-smooth if
\|x_{i}\|_{2}\leq 1
for all
i
. The gradient of
F
is

\nabla F(\theta)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}\nabla_{\hat{y}}\ell(\langle\theta,{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle,y_{i}){\bm{x}}_{i}.

Since
\ell(\hat{y},y)
is
L
-smooth in
\hat{y}
, for any
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
and any
i
, we have

\displaystyle\ell(\langle\theta_{2},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle,y_{i})-\ell(\langle\theta_{1},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle,y_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\nabla_{\hat{y}}\ell(\langle\theta_{1},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle,y_{i})\cdot(\langle\theta_{2},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle-\langle\theta_{1},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle)+\frac{L}{2}(\langle\theta_{2},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle-\langle\theta_{1},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle)^{2}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\hat{y}}\ell(\langle\theta_{1},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle,y_{i})\cdot{\bm{x}}_{i},\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}\rangle+\frac{L}{2}(\langle\theta_{2}-\theta_{1},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\hat{y}}\ell(\langle\theta_{1},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle,y_{i})\cdot{\bm{x}}_{i},\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\left\|\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle F(\theta_{2})-F(\theta_{1})=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}\left[\ell(\langle\theta_{2},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle,y_{i})-\ell(\langle\theta_{1},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle,y_{i})\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}\langle\nabla_{\hat{y}}\ell(\langle\theta_{1},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle,y_{i})\cdot{\bm{x}}_{i},\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}\left\|\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\nabla F(\theta_{1}),\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\left\|\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2},

which implies that
F(\theta)
is
L
-smooth.

Denote
\tilde{g}(\theta)=\nabla_{\hat{y}}\ell(f({\bm{X}};\theta),{\bm{Y}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then
\nabla F(\theta^{(t)})={\bm{X}}{\bm{Q}}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})
, and the update rule is

\theta^{(t+1)}=\theta^{(t)}-\eta{\bm{X}}{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)}).

(60)

So by
Definition

6.9
, we have

F(\theta^{(t+1)})\leq F(\theta^{(t)})-\eta\langle{\bm{X}}{\bm{Q}}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)}),{\bm{X}}{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\rangle+\frac{\eta^{2}L}{2}\left\|{\bm{X}}{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(61)

Let
\eta_{1}=\frac{q^{*}\lambda^{\min}}{2L(1+3\epsilon)\lambda^{\max}}
. Similar to the proof in
Appendix

12.A
, we can prove that for all
\eta\leq\eta_{1}
, and for all
t\geq t_{\epsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle F(\theta^{(t+1)})

\displaystyle\leq F(\theta^{(t)})-\frac{\eta q^{*}\lambda^{\min}}{2}\left\|\sqrt{{\bm{Q}}}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{\eta^{2}L}{2}\left\|{\bm{X}}\sqrt{{\bm{Q}}_{3\epsilon}^{(t)}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\sqrt{{\bm{Q}}}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq F(\theta^{(t)})-\frac{\eta q^{*}\lambda^{\min}}{2}\left\|\sqrt{{\bm{Q}}}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{\eta^{2}L}{2}\left\|{\bm{X}}\right\|_{2}^{2}(1+3\epsilon)\left\|\sqrt{{\bm{Q}}}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq F(\theta^{(t)})-\frac{\eta q^{*}\lambda^{\min}}{4}\left\|\sqrt{{\bm{Q}}}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq F(\theta^{(t)})-\frac{\eta q^{*2}\lambda^{\min}}{4}\left\|\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}.

This implies that
F(\theta^{(t)})
is monotonically non-increasing. Since
F(\theta)\geq 0
,
F(\theta^{(t)})
must converge as
t\rightarrow\infty
, and we need to prove that it converges to 0. Suppose that
F(\theta^{(t)})
does not converge to 0, then there exists a constant
C>0
such that
F(\theta^{(t)})\geq 2C
for all
t
. On the other hand, it is easy to see that there exists
\theta^{*}
such that
\ell(\langle\theta^{*},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle,y_{i})<C
for all
i
.
The above inequality also implies that
\left\|\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
because we must have
F(\theta^{(t)})-F(\theta^{(t+1)})\rightarrow 0
.

Note that by
Equation

60
, we have

\left\|\theta^{(t+1)}-\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\left\|\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}+2\eta\langle{\bm{X}}{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)}),\theta^{*}-\theta^{(t)}\rangle+\eta^{2}\left\|{\bm{X}}{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Define
F_{t}(\theta)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}^{(t)}\ell(\langle\theta,{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle,y_{i})
.

F_{t}
is convex because
\ell
is convex and
q_{i}^{(t)}
are non-negative, and
\nabla F_{t}(\theta^{(t)})={\bm{X}}{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})
. Convexity guarantees that
F_{t}({\bm{y}})\geq F_{t}({\bm{x}})+\langle\nabla F_{t}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{y}}-{\bm{x}}\rangle
, so for all
t
we have

\langle{\bm{X}}{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)}),\theta^{*}-\theta^{(t)}\rangle\leq F_{t}(\theta^{*})-F_{t}(\theta^{(t)})\leq F_{t}(\theta^{*})-\frac{2}{3}F(\theta^{(t)})\leq C-\frac{4C}{3}=-\frac{C}{3},

because
q_{i}^{(t)}\geq q_{i}-\epsilon\geq\frac{2}{3}q_{i}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}^{(t)}=1
. Since
\left\|\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}\rightarrow 0
, there exists
T>0
such that for all
t\geq T
and all
\eta\leq\eta_{0}
,

\left\|\theta^{(t+1)}-\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\left\|\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{\eta C}{3},

which means that
\left\|\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\rightarrow-\infty
because
\frac{\eta C}{3}
is a positive constant. This is a contradiction! Thus,
F(\theta^{(t)})
must converge to 0, which is result (i).

(i) immediately implies (ii) because
\ell
is strictly decreasing to 0 by the condition.

Now let us prove (iii).
First of all, the uniqueness of
\theta_{R}
can be easily proved from the convexity of
F(\theta)
. The condition implies that
y_{i}\langle\theta_{R},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle>0
, i.e.
\theta_{R}
must classify all training samples correctly. If there are two different minimizers
\theta_{R}
and
\theta_{R}^{\prime}
in whose norm is at most
R
, then consider
\theta_{R}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{1}{2}(\theta_{R}+\theta_{R}^{\prime})
. By the convexity of
F
, we know that
\theta_{R}^{\prime\prime}
must also be a minimizer, and
\|\theta_{R}^{\prime\prime}\|_{2}<R
. Thus,
F(\frac{R}{\|\theta_{R}^{\prime\prime}\|_{2}}\theta_{R}^{\prime\prime})<F(\theta_{R}^{\prime\prime})
and
\|\frac{R}{\|\theta_{R}^{\prime\prime}\|_{2}}\theta_{R}^{\prime\prime}\|_{2}=R
, which contradicts with the fact that
\theta_{R}^{\prime\prime}
is a minimizer.

To prove the rest of (iii), look at
Equation

61
. On one hand, for all
t\geq t_{\epsilon}
, we have

\left|\langle{\bm{X}}{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)}),{\bm{X}}({\bm{Q}}^{(t)}-{\bm{Q}})\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\rangle\right|\leq\lambda^{\max}\sqrt{3\epsilon}\left\|\sqrt{{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Since we chose
\epsilon=\min\{\frac{q^{*}}{3},\frac{(q^{*}\lambda^{\min})^{2}}{192\lambda^{\max 2}}\}
, this inequality implies that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla F_{t}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|{\bm{X}}{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq\lambda^{\min}\left\|{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq\lambda^{\min}(q^{*}-\epsilon)\left\|\sqrt{{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda^{\min}q^{*}}{2}\left\|\sqrt{{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq 4\left|\langle{\bm{X}}{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)}),{\bm{X}}({\bm{Q}}^{(t)}-{\bm{Q}})\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\rangle\right|.

On the other hand, if
\eta\leq\eta_{2}=\frac{1}{2L}
, then we have

\frac{\eta^{2}L}{2}\left\|{\bm{X}}{\bm{Q}}^{(t)}\tilde{g}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{\eta}{4}\left\|\nabla F_{t}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Combining the above with
Equation

61
, we get

F(\theta^{(t+1)})-F(\theta^{(t)})\leq-\frac{\eta}{2}\left\|\nabla F_{t}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Denote
{\bm{u}}=\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\theta_{R}}{\|\theta_{R}\|_{2}}
. Similar to Lemma 9 in
[
79
]
, we can prove that: for any
\alpha>0
, there exists a constant
\rho(\alpha)>0
such that for any
\theta
subject to
\|\theta\|_{2}\geq\rho(\alpha)
, the following holds for all
t
:

F_{t}((1+\alpha)\|\theta\|_{2}{\bm{u}})\leq F_{t}(\theta).

Let
t_{\alpha}\geq t_{\epsilon}
satisfy that for all
t\geq t_{\alpha}
,
\|\theta^{(t)}\|_{2}\geq\max\{\rho(\alpha),1\}
. By the convexity of
F_{t}
, for all
t\geq t_{\alpha}
, we have

\langle\nabla F_{t}(\theta^{(t)}),\theta^{(t)}-(1+\alpha)\|\theta^{(t)}\|_{2}{\bm{u}}\rangle\geq F_{t}(\theta^{(t)})-F_{t}((1+\alpha)\|\theta^{(t)}\|_{2}{\bm{u}})\geq 0.

(62)

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\langle\theta^{(t+1)}-\theta^{(t)},{\bm{u}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-\eta\nabla F_{t}(\theta^{(t)}),{\bm{u}}\rangle

(63)

\displaystyle\geq\langle-\eta\nabla F_{t}(\theta^{(t)}),\theta^{(t)}\rangle\frac{1}{(1+\alpha)\|\theta^{(t)}\|_{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle\theta^{(t+1)}-\theta^{(t)},\theta^{(t)}\rangle\frac{1}{(1+\alpha)\|\theta^{(t)}\|_{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\|\theta^{(t+1)}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left\|\theta^{(t)}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left\|\theta^{(t+1)}-\theta^{(t)}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\frac{1}{(1+\alpha)\|\theta^{(t)}\|_{2}}.

By
\frac{1}{2}(\|\theta^{(t+1)}\|_{2}-\|\theta^{(t)}\|_{2})^{2}\geq 0
, we have
(\frac{1}{2}\left\|\theta^{(t+1)}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left\|\theta^{(t)}\right\|_{2}^{2})/\|\theta^{(t)}\|_{2}\geq\left\|\theta^{(t+1)}\right\|_{2}-\left\|\theta^{(t)}\right\|_{2}
. Moreover, by
Equation

62
, we have

\frac{\left\|\theta^{(t+1)}-\theta^{(t)}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{2(1+\alpha)\|\theta^{(t)}\|_{2}}\leq\frac{\left\|\theta^{(t+1)}-\theta^{(t)}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{2}=\frac{\eta^{2}\left\|\nabla F_{t}(\theta^{(t)})\right\|_{2}^{2}}{2}\leq\eta\left(F(\theta^{(t)})-F(\theta^{(t+1)})\right).

Summing up
Equation

63
from
t=t_{\alpha}
to
t-1
yields

\langle\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{(t_{\alpha})},{\bm{u}}\rangle\geq\frac{\left\|\theta^{(t)}\right\|_{2}-\left\|\theta^{(t_{\alpha})}\right\|_{2}}{1+\alpha}+\eta\left(F(\theta^{(t)})-F(\theta^{(t_{\alpha})})\right)\geq\frac{\left\|\theta^{(t)}\right\|_{2}-\left\|\theta^{(t_{\alpha})}\right\|_{2}}{1+\alpha}-\eta F(\theta^{(t_{\alpha})}),

which implies that

\left\langle\frac{\theta^{(t)}}{\left\|\theta^{(t)}\right\|_{2}},{\bm{u}}\right\rangle\geq\frac{1}{1+\alpha}+\frac{1}{\left\|\theta^{(t)}\right\|_{2}}\left(\langle\theta^{(t_{\alpha})},{\bm{u}}\rangle-\frac{\|\theta^{(t_{\alpha})}\|_{2}}{1+\alpha}-\eta F(\theta^{(t_{\alpha})})\right).

Since
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|\theta^{(t)}\|_{2}=\infty
, we have

\liminf_{t\rightarrow\infty}\left\langle\frac{\theta^{(t)}}{\left\|\theta^{(t)}\right\|_{2}},{\bm{u}}\right\rangle\geq\frac{1}{1+\alpha}.

Since
\alpha
is arbitrary, we must have
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\theta^{(t)}}{\left\|\theta^{(t)}\right\|_{2}}={\bm{u}}
as long as
\eta\leq\min\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\}
.

Appendix 12.F
Analysis of the Logistic Loss

Here, we show that the logistic loss satisfies all the conditions in
Theorem

6.10
, and
\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\theta_{R}}{R}=\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}}
.

First, for the logistic loss we have
\nabla^{2}_{\hat{y}}\ell(\hat{y},y)=\frac{y^{2}}{e^{y\hat{y}}+e^{-y\hat{y}}+2}\leq\max_{i}\frac{y_{i}^{2}}{4}
, so
\ell
is smooth.

Second, let us analyze
\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\theta_{R}}{R}
.
For the logistic loss, it is easy to show that for any
\hat{\theta}^{\prime}\neq\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}}
, there exists an
R(\hat{\theta}^{\prime})>0
and an
\delta(\hat{\theta}^{\prime})>0
such that
F(R\cdot\theta)>F(R\cdot\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}})
for all
R\geq R(\hat{\theta}^{\prime})
and
\theta\in B(\hat{\theta}^{\prime},\delta(\hat{\theta}^{\prime}))
.
Let
S=\{\theta:\|\theta\|_{2}=1\}
. For any
\epsilon>0
,
S-B(\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}},\epsilon)
is a compact set. And for any
\theta\in S-B(\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}},\epsilon)
, there exist
R(\theta)
and
\delta(\theta)
as defined above. Thus, there must exist
\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{m}\in S-B(\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}},\epsilon)
such that
S-B(\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}},\epsilon)\subseteq\cup_{i=1}^{m}B(\theta_{i},\delta(\theta_{i}))
. Let
R(\epsilon)=\max\{R(\theta_{1}),\cdots,R(\theta_{m})\}
, then for all
R\geq R(\epsilon)
and all
\theta\in S-B(\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}},\epsilon)
,
F(R\cdot\theta)>F(R\cdot\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}})
, which means that
\frac{\theta_{R}}{R}\in B(\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}},\epsilon)
for all
R\geq R(\epsilon)
. Therefore,
\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\theta_{R}}{R}
exists and is equal to
\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}}
.

Appendix 12.G
Proof of Theorem
6.11

Proof

Let
M_{0}
be the bound of
{\left\|\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x)\right\|}_{2}
.
We first consider the regularized linearized neural network
f_{\textnormal{lin}\textrm{reg}}^{(t)}
.
By
Proposition

12.7
,
f^{(0)}({\bm{x}})
is sampled from a zero-mean Gaussian process, so there exists a constant
M>0
such that
|f^{(0)}({\bm{x}}_{i})|<M
for all
i
with high probability.
Define

F(\theta)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}\ell(\langle\theta,\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}({\bm{x}}_{i})\rangle+f^{(0)}({\bm{x}}_{i}),y_{i}).

Denote
\tilde{\theta}_{R}=\argmin_{\theta}\{F(R\cdot\theta):\|\theta\|_{2}\leq 1\}
.
when the linearized neural network is trained by GRW satisfying
Assumption

6.3
with regularization, since this is convex optimization and the objective function is smooth, we can prove that with a sufficiently small learning rate, as
t\rightarrow\infty
,
\theta^{(t)}\rightarrow R\cdot\tilde{\theta_{R}}+\theta^{(0)}
where
R=\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|\theta^{(t)}-\theta^{(0)}\|_{2}
(which is the minimizer). And define

\gamma=\min_{i=1,\cdots,n}y_{i}\cdot\langle\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}},\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x_{i})\rangle.

First, we derive the lower bound of
R
. By
Lemma

12.9
, with a sufficiently large
\tilde{d}
, with high probability
\hat{{\mathcal{R}}}(f_{\textrm{reg}}^{(t)})<\epsilon
implies
\hat{{\mathcal{R}}}(f_{\textnormal{lin}\textrm{reg}}^{(t)})<2\epsilon
. By the convexity of
\ell
, we have

\displaystyle 2\epsilon

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(\langle R\tilde{\theta}_{R},x_{i}\rangle+f^{(0)}(x_{i}),y_{i})\geq\log\left(1+\exp\left(-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\langle R\tilde{\theta}_{R},{\bm{x}}_{i}\rangle+f^{(0)}(x_{i}))y_{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\log\left(1+\exp\left(-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R\langle\tilde{\theta}_{R},x_{i}\rangle y_{i}-M\right)\right),

which implies that
R=\Omega(-\log 2\epsilon)
for all
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
.

Denote
\delta=\|\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}}-\tilde{\theta}_{R}\|_{2}
. Let
\theta^{\prime}=\frac{\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}}+\tilde{\theta}_{R}}{2}
, then we can see that
\|\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}=\sqrt{1-\frac{\delta^{2}}{4}}
. Let
\tilde{\theta}^{\prime}=\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{\|\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}}
. By the definition of
\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}}
, there exists
j
such that
y_{j}\cdot\langle\tilde{\theta}^{\prime},\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}({\bm{x}}_{j})\rangle\leq\gamma
, which implies that

y_{j}\cdot\left\langle\frac{\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}}+\tilde{\theta}_{R}}{2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{\delta^{2}}{4}}},\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}({\bm{x}}_{j})\right\rangle\leq\gamma.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle y_{j}\cdot\langle\tilde{\theta}_{R},\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x_{j})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{1-\frac{\delta^{2}}{4}}\gamma-y_{j}\cdot\langle\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}},\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}({\bm{x}}_{j})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\left(2\sqrt{1-\frac{\delta^{2}}{4}}-1\right)\gamma

\displaystyle\leq\left(2(1-\frac{\delta^{2}}{8})-1\right)\gamma\qquad(\text{since }\sqrt{1-x}\leq 1-\frac{x}{2})

\displaystyle=(1-\frac{\delta^{2}}{4})\gamma.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle q_{j}\log(1+\exp(-y_{j}\cdot\langle R\cdot\tilde{\theta}_{R},\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}({\bm{x}}_{j})\rangle-M))\leq F(R\cdot\tilde{\theta}_{R})

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle F(R\cdot\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}})\leq\log(1+\exp(-R\gamma+M)),

which implies that

q^{*}\log\left(1+\exp\left(-(1-\frac{\delta^{2}}{4})R\gamma-M\right)\right)\leq\log(1+\exp(-R\gamma+M)).

Thus, we have

1+\exp(-R\gamma+M)\geq\left(1+\exp\left(-(1-\frac{\delta^{2}}{4})R\gamma-M\right)\right)^{q^{*}}\geq 1+q^{*}\exp\left(-(1-\frac{\delta^{2}}{4})R\gamma-M\right),

which is equivalent to

-R\gamma+M\geq-(1-\frac{\delta^{2}}{4})R\gamma-M+\log(q^{*}).

From this, we conclude that

\delta=O(R^{-1/2})=O((-\log 2\epsilon)^{-1/2}).

So for any test point
x
such that
{\left\|\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x)\right\|}\leq M_{0}
, we have

{\left|{\left\langle\hat{\theta}_{\textnormal{MM}}-\tilde{\theta}_{R},\nabla_{\theta}f^{(0)}(x)\right\rangle}\right|}\leq\delta M_{0}=O((-\log 2\epsilon)^{-1/2}).

Combining this with
Lemma

12.9
, with high probability, we have

\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}|R\cdot f_{\textnormal{MM}}(x)-f_{\textrm{reg}}^{(t)}(x)|=O(R\cdot(-\log 2\epsilon)^{-1/2}+\tilde{d}^{-1/4}).

Hence, there exists a constant
C>0
such that: As
\tilde{d}\rightarrow\infty
, with high probability, for all
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
, if
|f_{\textnormal{MM}}(x)|>C\cdot(-\log 2\epsilon)^{-1/2}
, then
f_{\textrm{reg}}^{(t)}(x)
will have the same sign as
f_{\textnormal{MM}}(x)
for a sufficiently large
t
. Note that this
C
only depends on
n
,
q^{*}
,
\gamma
,
M
and
M_{0}
, so it is a constant independent of
\epsilon
.

Appendix 12.H
Proof of Proposition
6.16

Proof

We have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{D_{\beta},\rho,\epsilon}(\theta;P_{\rm{train}})

\displaystyle=\inf_{P^{\prime}}\left\{{\mathcal{R}}_{D_{\beta},\rho}(\theta;P^{\prime}):\exists\tilde{P^{\prime}}\text{ s.t. }P_{\rm{train}}=(1-\epsilon)P^{\prime}+\epsilon\tilde{P^{\prime}}\right\}

(64)

\displaystyle=\inf_{P^{\prime},\eta}\left\{c_{\beta}(\rho)\mathbb{E}_{P^{\prime}}[(\ell(\theta;Z)-\eta)_{+}^{\beta_{*}}]^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}}+\eta\right\}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\eta}\left\{c_{\beta}(\rho)\inf_{P^{\prime}}\{[\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}P^{\prime}((\ell(\theta;Z)-\eta)_{+}^{\beta_{*}}>u)du]^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}}\}+\eta\right\}.

Since
P_{\rm{train}}=(1-\epsilon)P^{\prime}+\epsilon\tilde{P^{\prime}}
, for all
\ell_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

P^{\prime}(\ell(\theta;Z)\leq\ell_{0})\leq\min\left\{1,\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}P_{\rm{train}}(\ell(\theta;Z)\leq\ell_{0})\right\},

and we can show that there exists a
P^{*}=P^{\prime}
that attains the equality for all
\ell_{0}
.
This is because
P_{\rm{train}}(\ell(\theta;z))
is a continuous function of
z
for any fixed
\theta
since both
\ell
and
P_{\rm{train}}
are continuous, so there exists an
\ell^{*}
such that
P_{\rm{train}}(\ell(\theta;Z)>\ell^{*})=\epsilon
.
Hence, we can define

P^{*}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}P_{\rm{train}}(z)&,\ell(\theta;z)\leq\ell^{*};\\
0&,\ell(\theta;z)>\ell^{*}.\end{array}\right.

For this
P^{*}
, we have
\int_{{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}}P^{*}(z)dz=\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\int_{\ell(\theta;z)<\ell^{*}}P_{\rm{train}}(z)dz=\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}P_{\rm{train}}(\ell(\theta;Z)<\ell^{*})=1
because
P_{\rm{train}}(\ell(\theta;Z)=\ell^{*})=0
, which means that
P^{*}
is a proper probability density function.

Let
v=u^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}}
. Plugging
P^{*}(\ell(\theta;Z)\leq\ell_{0})=\min\left\{1,\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}P_{\rm{train}}(\ell(\theta;Z)\leq\ell_{0})\right\}
into
Equation

64
produces

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{D_{\beta},\rho,\epsilon}(\theta;P_{\rm{train}})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\eta}\left\{c_{\beta}(\rho)\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}[1-P^{*}((\ell(\theta;Z)-\eta)_{+}^{\beta_{*}}\leq v^{\beta_{*}})]dv^{\beta_{*}}\right]^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}}+\eta\right\}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\eta}\left\{c_{\beta}(\rho)\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}[1-\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}P_{\rm{train}}(\ell(\theta;Z)\leq\eta+v)]_{+}dv^{\beta_{*}}\right]^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}}+\eta\right\}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\eta}\left\{c_{\beta}(\rho)\left[\int_{0}^{(\ell^{*}-\eta)_{+}}\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}[(1-\epsilon)-P_{\rm{train}}(\ell(\theta;Z)\leq\eta+v)]_{+}dv^{\beta_{*}}\right]^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}}+\eta\right\}.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{\rm{train}}}[(\ell-\eta)_{+}^{\beta_{*}}\mid P_{Z^{\prime}\sim P_{\rm{train}}}(\ell(\theta;Z^{\prime})>\ell(\theta;Z))\geq\epsilon]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\ell^{*}}(u-\eta)_{+}^{\beta_{*}}d(P_{\rm{train}}(\ell\leq u))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\left\{\left[(u-\eta)_{+}^{\beta_{*}}P_{\rm{train}}(\ell\leq u)\right]_{0}^{\ell^{*}}-\int_{0}^{\ell^{*}}P_{\rm{train}}(\ell\leq u)d((u-\eta)_{+}^{\beta_{*}})\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\left\{(\ell^{*}-\eta)_{+}^{\beta_{*}}(1-\epsilon)-\int_{0}^{\ell^{*}}P_{\rm{train}}(\ell\leq u)d((u-\eta)_{+}^{\beta_{*}})\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\left\{\int_{0}^{(\ell^{*}-\eta)_{+}}(1-\epsilon)dv^{\beta_{*}}-\int_{0}^{(\ell^{*}-\eta)_{+}}P_{\rm{train}}(\ell\leq\eta+w)dw^{\beta_{*}}\right\},

where
w=(u-\eta)_{+}
. This completes the proof.

Remark 12.12
.

We can prove a similar dual formula even if
P_{\rm{train}}
is not continuous.
For any
P_{\rm{train}}
, there exists an
\ell^{*}
such that
P_{\rm{train}}(\ell(\theta;Z)>\ell^{*})\leq\epsilon
and
P_{\rm{train}}(\ell(\theta;Z)<\ell^{*})\leq 1-\epsilon
.
If
P_{\rm{train}}(\ell(\theta;Z)=\ell^{*})=0
, then we still define
P^{*}
the same as in the above proof.
If
P_{\rm{train}}(\ell(\theta;Z)=\ell^{*})>0
, then we define

P^{*}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}P_{\rm{train}}(z)&,\ell(\theta;z)<\ell^{*};\\
\left[1-\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}P_{\rm{train}}(\ell(\theta;Z)<\ell^{*})\right]/P_{\rm{train}}(\ell(\theta;Z)=\ell^{*})&,\ell(\theta;z)=\ell^{*};\\
0&,\ell(\theta;z)>\ell^{*},\end{array}\right.

with which the dual formula becomes

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{D_{\beta},\rho,\epsilon}(\theta;P_{\rm{train}})

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\inf_{\eta}\{c_{\beta}(\rho)(\frac{P_{\rm{train}}(\ell<\ell^{*})}{1-\epsilon}\mathbb{E}_{Z}[(\ell(\theta;Z)-\eta)_{+}^{\beta_{*}}\mid P_{Z^{\prime}}(\ell(\theta;Z^{\prime})>\ell(\theta;Z))>\epsilon]

\displaystyle+\frac{1-P_{\rm{train}}(\ell<\ell^{*})}{1-\epsilon}(\ell^{*}-\eta)_{+}^{\beta_{*}})^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}}+\eta\}.

Appendix 12.I
Proof of Theorem
6.17

The proof relies on the following key technical lemma.

Lemma 12.13
.

For any distributions
P,P^{\prime}
, non-negative loss function
l(\cdot,Z)
and
1\leq\beta_{*}<2k
, such that
\mathbb{E}_{P}[l(\theta,Z)^{2k}]<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[(\ell-\eta)^{\beta_{*}}_{+}]^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}}\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P^{\prime}}[(\ell-\eta)^{\beta_{*}}_{+}]^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}}+\mathbb{E}_{P}[(l(\theta,Z)-\eta)_{+}^{2k}]^{\frac{1}{2k}}\textnormal{TV}(P,P^{\prime})^{\left(\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}-\frac{1}{2k}\right)}\beta_{*}^{-\frac{1}{2k}}\cdot\left(\frac{2k}{2k-\beta_{*}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}}

Proof

By the definition of the total variation distance, we have
P(\ell(\theta;Z)>u)-P^{\prime}(\ell(\theta;Z^{\prime})>u)\leq\textnormal{TV}(P,P^{\prime})
for all
u\geq 0
.
Let
s_{2k}:=\mathbb{E}[(\ell-\eta)_{+}^{2k}]^{\frac{1}{2k}}
.
By Markov’s inequality and the non-negativity of
\ell
, we have

P(\ell-\eta>u)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[(\ell-\eta)_{+}^{2k}]}{u^{2k}}:=(\frac{s_{2k}}{u})^{2k}\qquad\text{for all }\eta\geq 0.

(65)

Using integration by parts, we have

\mathbb{E}_{P}[(\ell-\eta)^{\beta_{*}}_{+}]=\int_{\eta}^{\infty}\beta_{*}(t-\eta)^{(\beta_{*}-1)}P(\ell\geq t)dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\beta_{*}u^{(\beta_{*}-1)}P(\ell-\eta\geq u)du.

This implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[(\ell-\eta)^{\beta_{*}}_{+}]-\mathbb{E}_{P^{\prime}}[(\ell-\eta)^{\beta_{*}}_{+}]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\beta_{*}u^{(\beta_{*}-1)}\left(P(\ell-\eta\geq u)-P^{\prime}(\ell-\eta\geq u)\right)du

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{M}+\int_{M}^{\infty}\right)\left(\beta_{*}u^{(\beta_{*}-1)}\left(P(\ell-\eta\geq u)-P^{\prime}(\ell-\eta\geq u)\right)du\right).

Here,
M
is a positive parameter whose value will be determined later. For the first integral, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{M}\beta_{*}u^{(\beta_{*}-1)}\left(P(\ell-\eta\geq u)-P^{\prime}(\ell-\eta\geq u)\right)du

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{M}\beta_{*}u^{(\beta_{*}-1)}\textnormal{TV}(P,P^{\prime})du

\displaystyle=M^{\beta_{*}}\textnormal{TV}(P,P^{\prime}).

For the second integral, by
Equation

65
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}^{\infty}\beta_{*}u^{(\beta_{*}-1)}\left(P(\ell-\eta\geq u)-P^{\prime}(\ell-\eta\geq u)\right)du

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}^{\infty}\beta_{*}u^{(\beta_{*}-1)}P(\ell-\eta\geq u)du

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}^{\infty}\beta_{*}u^{(\beta_{*}-1)}\left(\frac{s_{2k}}{u}\right)^{2k}

\displaystyle=\frac{s_{2k}^{2k}}{2k-\beta_{*}}\cdot\frac{1}{M^{2k-\beta_{*}}}.

Therefore, by setting
M=s_{2k}(\textnormal{TV}(P,P^{\prime})\beta_{*})^{-1/2k}
which minimizes the sum of two terms, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[(\ell-\eta)^{\beta_{*}}_{+}]-\mathbb{E}_{P^{\prime}}[(\ell-\eta)^{\beta_{*}}_{+}]\leq

\displaystyle\inf_{M>0}\left(M^{\beta_{*}}\textnormal{TV}(P,P^{\prime})+\frac{s_{2k}^{2k}}{2k-\beta_{*}}\cdot\frac{1}{M^{2k-\beta_{*}}}\right)=s_{2k}^{\beta_{*}}\textnormal{TV}(P,P^{\prime})^{1-\frac{\beta_{*}}{2k}}\beta_{*}^{-\frac{\beta_{*}}{2k}}\cdot\frac{2k}{2k-\beta_{*}}.

Using the inequality
(A+B)^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}}\leq A^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}}+B^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}}
when
\beta_{*}\geq 1
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{P}[(\ell-\eta)^{\beta_{*}}_{+}]^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}}\leq\mathbb{E}_{P^{\prime}}[(\ell-\eta)^{\beta_{*}}_{+}]^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}}+s_{2k}\textnormal{TV}(P,P^{\prime})^{\left(\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}-\frac{1}{2k}\right)}\beta_{*}^{-\frac{1}{2k}}\cdot\left(\frac{2k}{2k-\beta_{*}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta_{*}}},

as desired.

Now we prove
Theorem

6.17
.

Proof

By Lemma
12.13
, for any
P^{\prime}
such that
\textnormal{TV}(P,P^{\prime})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}
, we have

\textnormal{CVaR}_{\alpha}(\theta;P)-\textnormal{CVaR}_{\alpha}(\theta;P^{\prime})\leq 2\alpha^{-1}\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}}.

By
Corollary

6.13
, if
{\mathcal{R}}_{\max}(\theta;P)>3\alpha^{-1}\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}}
, then
\textnormal{CVaR}_{\alpha}(\theta;P)>3\alpha^{-1}\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}}
, which implies that

\frac{\textnormal{CVaR}_{\alpha}(\theta;P^{\prime})}{{\mathcal{R}}_{\max}(\theta;P)}\geq\frac{\textnormal{CVaR}_{\alpha}(\theta;P^{\prime})}{\textnormal{CVaR}_{\alpha}(\theta;P)}=1-\frac{\delta}{\textnormal{CVaR}_{\alpha}(\theta;P)}\geq 1-\frac{2\alpha^{-1}\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}}}{3\alpha^{-1}\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}}}=\frac{1}{3}

holds for any
P^{\prime}
such that
\textnormal{TV}(P,P^{\prime})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}
. By
Lemma

6.15
, taking the infimum over
P^{\prime}
yields the first inequality of
Equation

35
.
And by
Corollary

6.13
, we have
D_{\chi^{2},\rho}(\theta;P^{\prime})\geq\textnormal{CVaR}_{\alpha}(\theta;P^{\prime})
for all
\theta
and
P^{\prime}
.
This combined with the above inequality yields the second inequality of
Equation

35
.
